## Fallen Kingdom (disambiguation)

The Fallen Kingdom may refer to:
 Illefarn, the ancient elven realm of northwest Faerûn that disbanded in 342 DR, or
 The alliance of Illefarn between the elves of Ardeep and the dwarves of Dardath from 342 DR to 523 DR, or
 Phalorm, the Realm of Three Crowns that existed from 523 DR to 615 DR, or
 Delimbiyran, the Kingdom of Man that succeeded Phalorm from 616 DR to 697 DR, or
 The brief, second alliance of Illefarn between the moon elves of Ardeep Forest, the humans in the Delimbiyr river valley, and the dwarves of the Forlorn Hills in 882 DR.

## Farwater

The Farwater was the largely uncultivated region of Impiltur, located in the northeastern part of the realm beyond the Great Imphras River. It was a majestically beautiful but uncivilized locale that drew in Impilturan settlers and frontiersmen to its numerous fledgling farming communities.

Many of the region's pioneers hired adventuring parties to protect them from the ever-present threat of hobgoblins that poured down from the Earthspur Mountains.

Notable Locations
Bay Town: Located on the shores of the Bluefang Water, this large settlement was rife with crime led by a large thieves' guilds.
Ilmwatch: This imposing fortress-town guarded the trade route that led from southern Impiltur, north along the Herald's Run to the realm of Damara.

## Faces of Evil: The Fiends

Faces of Evil: The Fiends  is a 2-edition Advanced Dungeons & Dragons accessory. It is part of the Planescape campaign setting.

Faces of Evil: The Fiends contains detailed descriptions and statistics for a wide range of fiends, from lowly imp minions to powerful archdevils and demon lords. It also includes information on fiendish cults, the politics of the lower planes, and advice on how to run campaigns focused on battling the forces of evil.

The book is intended for use by Dungeon Masters and players who want to explore the darker aspects of the game's world and challenge their characters with dangerous and diabolical foes.

## Fatigue

Fatigue was an enchantment spell that induced exhaustion and weariness within any person or creature that passed through an affected area.

Effects
Upon casting this spell, a magical zone extended outward from the caster in the shape of a 60' (18.3) long cone, with a 30' (9.1 m) wide base. Any being caught within the area immediately succumbed to intense fatigue. Their movement became sluggish, their reflexes were hampered and their spirit and determination were drained from their being.

The duration of this spell lasted for a matter of seconds, but increased depending on the skill and experience of the caster.

Usage
This spell was unique to the elves of Evermeet. The specifications of its casting were a well-kept secret of island realm.

Components
In addition to verbal and somatic components, this spell required the material component of a small stone.

## Falaeve

Highlord Falaeve was the sun elf ruler of Siluvanede around the 3 century DR.

Activities
In keeping with Siluvanedenn haughtiness, Falaeve forbade all visitors to the realm, not even "lesser" races of elves. The Highlord made this point coldly clear to all. The only place he permitted other races was at the city of Lothen, where they came to consult the Aeltagarr. He was of course dismissive of the human realm of Athalantar and its magelords.

History
In the Year of the Flaming Forests, 224 DR, a figure purporting to be Highlord Falaeve visited Morlin Castle, at a feast hosted by Lord Breiyr and also attended by Axelord Arthlach of Ammarindar and a Herald of Tavaray known as Huntinghorn. Huntinghorn reported on how Athalantar's forces had used magic to burn forests and slaughter elves across the Northlands, even destroying the Halangorn Forest west of Morlin Castle. Yet Falaeve was strangely dubious and dismissive and tried covertly casting a spell. For that, Arthlach slew Falaeve, who was revealed to be Mage Royal Ubriien Orlyn of Athalantar in disguise, doubtless the one responsible for the attacks on Siluvanede. Huntinghorn, meanwhile, was in fact the adventuress Myrjala in magical disguise as well. The real Highlord Falaeve was never present.

Description
The false Falaeve displayed sharp elven features, large silver eyes, bronze skin, a hawkish nose, and hair that was long and golden-blond in hue; his expression was often haughty or sneering. He was tall and graceful. He wore a white silk long-sleeved shirt and an overtunic of gilden shimmerweave, with bracers decorated with gems and the silver griffon symbol of Siluvanede worked in gold wire, and kept his hair tied back tightly at the nape of his neck with a filigreed pin adorned with fine gems and the dazzle of spells. It is unknown what of this disguise represented the real Highlord.

Notes

## Faithful mount

Faithful mount was a charm spell, within the greater enchantment school of magic, that allowed a cleric to enjoy a strong and permanent bond with a mount of their choosing.

Effects
Upon casting this spell, a specific mount was placed under the effects of the spells charm mammal and speak with animals. They became exceptionally loyal to the spell's caster and could converse with and understand them as if they spoke the same language. While the effects of the spell were permanent, it was possible that the targeted animal could resist its effects, whereby it was permanently immune to any further castings by the same cleric or priest.

This spell affected a number of animals that could be ridden as mounts including mules, donkeys, ponies, horses, rhinoceroses, elephants, hippogriffs, griffons, pegasi, and even unicorns.

Components
In addition to verbal and somatic components, this spell required a small lump of sugar in order to be cast.

## Falx

Falx was a large planet with a high population of mind flayers and speculated by some scholars to be the original homeworld of the tarrasque species.

Description
From space, Falx appeared like a featureless white sphere. This was a result of constant, thick cloud cover, which masked the barren landscape below.

The planet was roughly  in radius, with a circumference about two-and-half times as large as Toril's at the equator.

The nearly perfectly round planet had a day length of 30 hours. Falx had no magnetic field, so compasses were useless there. Because of the thick cloud cover, it was never very bright during the day, and at night, it was utterly black. The dim daytime sky of Falx was green-blue, because the clouds absorbed more red light than a planet such as Toril.

Geography
Falx had a thicker crust than most other planets of its size with a smaller central core of nickel and iron. This led to less volcanic activity than other Earth worlds. There were no active volcanoes, hot springs, or other geothermal features.

The surface was relatively flat, with smoothed hills but no substantial mountains. The change in elevation from the highest point to the lowest point on the planet was only . The planet showed evidence of previous meteor strikes in its history, in the form of craters. These craters were postulated to have once been filled with water. The three largest such basins were called the Deadsea Basin, the Firestrike Basin, and Catastrophe Basin. Since all such "seas" were now dried up, the planet consisted of a single continent. In fact, there was no permanent liquid water anywhere on the planet, all water was found in the heavy cloud cover or deep beneath the surface.

Below the surface, there was evidence of ancient volcanic activity, in the form of massive networks of hollow lava tubes and subterranean lakes filling massive caves.

Climate

Like Toril, the axis was tilted, granting seasons to the planet; however, the effect of the seasons on climate and temperature was minimal.

The atmosphere had a high percentage of carbon dioxide, leading to a greenhouse effect that kept the planet very warm, about  on average. The temperatures were even hotter at the equator but cooler at the poles, although even at the poles, it was still so hot that water falling from the clouds as rain never reached the surface.

The high levels of carbon dioxide meant that it was harder to maintain a natural fire. It was also harder for most humanoid creatures to breathe, and such beings grew tired quickly on Falx.

Lightning storms often flashed in the lower cloud levels, acting as strobe lights. The clouds began at an elevation of about —where the temperature was about —and extended upwards again about that distance. Winds were extremely strong on Falx, reaching hurricane speeds often.

Flora & Fauna
While sages speculated that Falx once had water and life on its surface, for all of recorded history, the planet was almost barren. The only notable surface flora were called "carpet mosses" because of their appearance and feel. These mosses formed colonies that could reach hundreds of feet in width and were mobile. Most were red or orange in color, but there were many distinct species.

All animal life was approximately reptilian. Most of these small animals were under a yard (meter) in length and had four legs, but some also had six or eight. Most were venomous scavengers who fed on both mosses and other lizards. There were many varieties, such as the Falx imbul.

While the smaller animals and plants survived the heat and winds by hiding, the other major type of life form was simply large enough and powerful enough to resist such hazards. Falx was home to a population of several hundred creatures that were either tarrasques or creatures extremely similar to them. Some sages argued that the idea of the tarrasque being a unique creature was a falsehood and that the tarrasque of Toril was simply a specimen of the creatures from Falx that had been transported to Toril by some means unknown.

Inhabitants
All sentient lifeforms on Falx lived below the surface. By far, the most populous of these were illithids, although orcs and goblinoids also had a limited population. These latter two groups were used by the mind flayers as slaves and food. There was also a relatively large population of myconids.

Rumors existed of other sentient life below the surface, including marooned populations of dwarves and drow and even reports of aboleths in the deepest watery reaches of the planet.

Notable Locations
The two major illithid cities of Falx were called Brain-in-the-Skull and Tentacles-Grip-Tightly, when directly translated into Common. Each was ruled by a mind flayer wizard rumored to have been trained by an alliance with the supposed aboleths. The former city, with a population of over two million, was in the northern hemisphere; the latter in the south, with a population of just under one million. The elder brain of Brain-in-the-Skull was so developed that its telepathic range was over ten miles (sixteen kilometers)!

Both cities cooperated with each other, and in addition, there were numerous other outposts scattered about the caverns of the planet.

The illithids of Falx were not in any way ignorant of spelljamming technology, but for the most part, they kept to their own planet and considered how to dominate the other races living there. (A notable exception to this trend is discussed in the History section below.) The illithids defended their planet from any intruders, using such magics as astral projection and levitation to take out any ships approaching the surface.

History
In, illithids from Falx had heard about the Cloak of the First Pilot and were actively seeking it. They hired a mercenary named Barrab to apprehend the cloak from Teldin Moore upon his arrival on Toril, and their own vessels had even come as far as the orbit of Toril, where they happened upon the illithid historian Estriss, who had been lost in space after a battle with the neogi Prissith Nerro on board the Arcane vessel Nebulon. Estriss was taken aboard as a guest.

Some time later, the same illithids were still in Realmspace, near the planet Garden. Let by the illithid Netarza, they had captured an elven man-o-war and were attempting to learn more about the location of the dracon homeworld, when they had an encounter with Teldin Moore, which ended poorly for them.

Rumors & Legends
Some said that the aboleths were seeking a way off the planet and were working with the illithids to have their own spelljammers and spelljamming helms built for them.

## Faelas Xorlarrin

Faelas Xorlarrin was a drow wizard of House Xorlarrin in Menzoberranzan in the late 15 century DR.

History
Faelas was a competent Xorlarrin wizard. In the, he was a master of Sorcere and a coded letter was written to him about how Lolth's current plan supported the idea of wizards joining the Ruling Council.

In the, Faelas and his cousin and fellow Sorcere master Jaemas Xorlarrin accompanied the demon army led by Marilith and Nalfeshnee. They witnessed the defeat of the two demon lords at the hands of Drizzt Do'Urden, and Jarlaxle urged them to report the news to Matron Mother Zeerith Q'Xorlarrin.

In the, Faelas was not chosen to join the reformed House Do'Urden.

## Face

Face was a common spell used by wu jen in Kara-Tur. The spell was very rare outside Kara-Tur.

Effects
This spell was a combination of illusion and charm. When cast, it gave a wu jen the illusion of a higher social status. During casting, the wu jen decided which standing was increased, either social status, title, or office. The spell caused the wu jen to display higher levels of confidence and sophistication to those around them. The spell also gave the wu jen the appropriate mannerisms, aura of power, speech, and etiquette for the assumed position.

The spell worked best on those who were unfamiliar with the assumed rank. They treated the wu jen with great respect and respected their authority. For those who were familiar with the assumed rank, there was a risk they would become suspicious and see through the illusion and charm and the spell would be rendered useless.

The reverse of the spell was lose face.

Components
In addition to verbal and somatic components, the spell required a material component, a single drop of honey.

## Face-blur

Face-blur was a common illusion spell used by ninjas. The spell was very rare outside Kara-Tur.

Effects
A ninja was able to cast this spell on themselves or another person. If cast on another person, that person had to be willing to participate in the spell. The spell disguised the target by altering their face so that they appeared utterly unremarkable, and increased their ability to blend in with any crowd. Anyone who looked at the target forgot any identifying features.

The spell was not always effective. It could not hide the appearance of a target from someone actively looking into a crowd, such as an attentive guard. The spell depended on the target to maintain an aura of dullness. Deliberately drawing attention to oneself caused the spell to expire. The spell was vulnerable to dispel magic.

Components
The spell required only a somatic component, kuji-kiri hand gestures.

Appendix
References

---

## False tracks

False tracks was a common illusion spell used by ninjas to elude pursuers. The spell was very rare outside Kara-Tur.

Effects
When cast, the spell allowed a ninja to hide their tracks on a soft surface such as dirt or dust. The spell made the ninja's footprints appear as normal animal tracks. The ninja choose which animal during casting. The tracks adjusted for the speed of the ninja's movement. The spell's illusion extended to the clothing of the ninja, so if a piece of the ninja's clothing was cut off or snagged, it appeared as a piece of animal skin. The illusion did not apply to the ninja's equipment or items.

The spell was vulnerable to dispel magic. If a dispel magic spell was cast anywhere on the track, the entire illusion would shatter, thus revealing the ninja's true footprints. When the spell expired, the tracks reverted to normal.

Components
The spell required only a somatic component, kuji-kiri hand gestures.

References

## Faction War

Faction War is a 2-edition Advanced Dungeons & Dragons adventure module. It is part of the Planescape campaign setting.

Description
Faction War was written by Monte Cook and was published by TSR, Inc. in 1998.

The adventure module is centered around the ongoing conflict between the various factions that operate within the city of Sigil. The factions are organizations with their own unique goals, beliefs, and agendas, and each seeks to exert influence over the city and the planes of existence that it connects to.

Synopsis
The culmination of several adventures leading up to that point, The Faction War brought an end to the factions' control of the city. Instigated by the power-hungry Duke Rowan Darkwood, factol of the Fated, in a bid to dethrone the Lady of Pain and rule Sigil himself, the war spread throughout the city before the Lady of Pain, with the aid of a group of adventurers (the players' characters), intervened.

## Fantastic machine

Fantastic machine was a divine illusion spell that created an illusory and very noisy mechanical contraption.
This spell was attributed to Gond.

Users
It was solely available to clerics who worshiped deities of the Craft or Gnome domains.

Effect
When cast, this spell brought into being a large mechanical construct. It was illusory but very noisy and had an imposing and massive build and many arms. It had a hard, armored casing but was not especially resilient. It trundled along the ground at a pace one-third faster than a human. It was capable of swimming or flying, but did so slowly and clumsily.

It could be commanded by the spellcaster to carry out and repeat a simple, physical task, but instructions could be no more than twenty-five words in length. Instructions were given only when the spell was cast and could not be changed.

It could carry up to, but moved slowly when fully loaded, and it could not fly or swim if carrying over . It could lift a weight of up to  to a height of  and push or drag . The fantastic machine could also excavate a volume of loose rock up to  per minute; sand or loose soil could be moved twice as fast.

The machine attacked by slamming one of its arms against an opponent; it was thrice as effective against metal or stone as it was against creatures and other materials. It could throw rocks or similar masses of a certain size up to a  with ease, and up to  in total.

## Farr Windward

Farr Windward was a small settlement close to Orlumbor; the population was strictly limited to outcasts, eccentrics, and their descendants, from many cities of the Sword Coast.

Geography
Farr Windward was to the west of Orlumbor, on the other side of a long, winding, mountainous tunnel trail. Due to the treacherous, sunless, and monster-filled path, it could take adventurers days to reach the high-up village.

The village was built upon a rocky peninsula, atop soaring cliffs. As the far descent to the sea below was very rocky, escaping was almost impossible. The only other way out was through the vigilant guards, or death via "The Leap".

Government
The state did not seem to have its own government. Instead, it was maintained by the major cities of the Sword Coast. However, the small settlement had some citizens that appeared to lead and guide the others.

Trade
Although Farr Windward did not have the allowance of free trade, many residents invented and sold useful items, such as magical boot liners, known as Farrberjiks.

History
In 1358 DR, the inhabitants had finally had enough of being treated like prisoners. Thus, two residents, Haalbok and Ougo, had devised a plan to free the people of Farr Windward forever. After recruiting the Heroes of Ascore and dispatching the opposing spy network, they acquired the branding iron that marked those as being of the colony of Farr Windward. Ougo and the heroes made their way out of the the colony and branded the leader of Orlumbor, Tulgar Wrighttson. Wrighttson would have been declared to be banished and sent to the colony with the misfits. However, Ougo offered a solution, and Wrighttson subsequently nullified the meaning of the brand, and freed the colony. He allowed Farr Windward to become a free community in Orlumbor.

Inhabitants

All in the small community had been banished from their original settlements, either due to their power, unconventional lifestyles, or craziness. Guards roamed the streets, and kept records of the inmates.

Every inhabitant of Farr Windward had a large red-circle brand on their forearm. The brand indicated that they were part of the community, and could never leave. The brand also symbolized that, if they were able to miraculously escape, they were deranged and unable to fit in with society. If one tried to leave, or helped them to leave, the penalty was death.

## Faerinaal's hymn

Named for the consort to the eladrin queen Morwel, Faerinaal's hymn was an enchantment spell for bards that prevented opportunistic attacks by evil creatures. The spell was granted to the Celebrants of Sharess by their goddess.

Effect
By playing a gentle hymn, the spell clouded the minds of evil beings in close range who heard it, making them forget to attack when an opportunity presented itself. It lasted as long as the bard concentrated and continued playing it.

Components

## Fair Seas Festival

The Fair Seas Festival was a two-day-long festival held in Waterdeep in the last two days of Ches. It marked the end of the Fleetswake festival and encompassed most of the holiday's religious significance.

Description
The festival was intended more as a prevention ritual rather than a celebration. It was meant to protect Waterdeep from the wrath of Umberlee.

Activities
During the first day of the festival, the main activities were feasts in all of the city's taverns, almost exclusively involving seafood.

During both days, City Guard officers and members of both the Guild of Watermen and the Master Mariners' Guild toured the city from tavern to tavern collecting donations for Umberlee. Collection boxes were also placed at large gatherings for the festival and at both guilds' Guildhalls.

On the second day, clerics from all temples in the city and members of the sea-related guilds spent the entire day on Deepwater Isle praying and fasting. At dawn and dusk, Deepwater Harbor was decorated with flower petals in homage to Umberlee.

At sunset on the second day, all donations were placed in chests and cast into the deepest part of the harbor. The treasure was added to Umberlee's Cache, an enormous treasure pile that was closely guarded by merfolk and surrounded by myth and legend.

History
In the mid‒14 century DR, a temporary structure was built over the waters of Smugglers' Dock in the lower Castle Ward during the holiday. It was used for a feast that Piergeiron, Mirt, and other nobles involved in maritime activities shared with the merfolk inhabitants of Deepwater Harbor.

By the, it was estimated that the amount of gold donated to Umberlee's Cache had surpassed 2,500,000 gp.

Appearances

## Fazrian

Fazrian was an evil planetar who ruled the Terminus Level of Undermountain.

History
Fazrian originally served Torm, until being called down by a cleric in Undermountain.

Arriving there, Fazrian was disgusted by the corruption and sought to cleanse it. Fazrian failed to realize it was slowly becoming more bloodthirsty and eventually slaughtered the cleric who had summoned it, along with her companions.

Fazrian then proceeded to take control of the Terminus Level. Halaster Blackcloak sent Fazrian a host of four yugoloths to command, in exchange for payment. The four included the arcanaloths Aximus and Exekarus, the ultroloth Xindulus, and the nycaloth Raxxus.

## Favric

Favric was the local group leader of the Cult of the Dragon in Thundertree circa 1491 DR.

Description
Favric dressed in the typical garb of a dragon cultist, wearing a long dark robe and a black leather mask. He could speak Draconic, as well as Common. The young leader was far more ambitious than others of his sect. Favric wore a necklace that was actually a potion of flying attached a band.

History
Around 1491 DR, Favric led five other members of the cult to Thundertree, where they hoped to parlay with the green dragon, Venomfang. The group found a dusty, abandoned home to the southeast of the village. Since then, they tried to determine the needs of the young dragon, so they might broker a deal. Favric had brought three diamonds to appease the beast.

## Falling wall

Falling wall was an arcane evocation spell that created a temporary wall composed of armored plates that fell from above.

Effects
When cast, this spell caused 4-inch-thick (10 centimeters) plates of armor to "fall" into place in a line 20 feet long by 20 feet high (6.1 meters by 6.1 meters). The wall only lasted a short time before melting away into nothingness. While in effect, the wall presented an immovable object that could withstand any amount of force, but could be removed by spells such as dispel magic or disintegrate. Most other spells bounced off the wall or were forced to go around it if they could.

A falling wall could be used to damage a creature or object if it landed on it when cast. If a creature struck the wall with uncontrolled force (such as running into it at full speed) it would also suffer damage.

The spell did not require concentration to maintain.

Components
Falling wall had both verbal and somatic components. It also required a piece of armor that had been worn into battle or a piece of tempered metal as a material component.

History
During one of their many spell-sharing sessions, Elminster shared this spell with the wizards Mordenkainen of Oerth and Dalamar of Krynn.

## Fang fist

Fang fist was a spell that was commonly used by alhoons.

Effects
When cast, one of the caster's lower arms was transformed into a flexible appendage with a mouth at the end. The appendage resembled a snake. The mouth was filled with razor-sharp fangs. The length of the appendage was variable, from 1–14 feet (0.3–4.3 meters) in length. It could extend to its full length with incredible speed. The appendage's strength was the same as the caster's. The caster was able to use the appendage's mouth to grasp items. This spell could not be combined with any poison-based spells. While it was in effect, the caster could only case spells which had only a verbal component.

Components
This spell required only verbal and somatic components.

## False dawn

False dawn was a spell that was commonly used by clerics of Lathander.

Effects
A caster used this spell to mimic a normal rising of the sun. The spell created a bright light that resembled the rising sun. The light was bright enough to dispel darkness-based spells. This spell not only harmed creatures sensitive to light, but also inflicted a state of confusion upon them. Undead creatures were particularly vulnerable to this spell. If this spell killed an undead creature, it did not reanimate. Creatures who used infravision were unable to do so for a short period after exposure to the light from this spell.

Components
In addition to verbal and somatic components, the spell required a material component: a gem with a value of at least 1000 gp. The gemstone could be red, transparent, or yellow in hue. It was consumed during casting.

## Faltrax

Faltrax was an incubus of the late 15 century DR.

History
When Elturel was transported to Avernus, first layer of the Nine Hells, in the, Faltrax infiltrated the city on a mission to acquire intelligence on its defenses, planning to sell this to whichever fiend paid the highest. It posed as a male human blacksmith named Orin Ragron, with a shop in the city, but was attacked by two bulezaus who'd also found their way in. Faltrax would try to enlist the aid adventurers, but would flee if found out.It is unknown if Orin Ragron was a real person in Elturel.

## Faith armor

Faith armor was a divine spell that protected an individual from physical and magical attacks.

Effects
When this spell was cast, it surrounded the recipient in a translucent barrier that offered significant protection. For a limited time, it made them more resilient to physical attacks and protected them from spells that fell under a single school of magic or divine sphere.

History
The title of this spell was inscribed within the Helm of Helm and could be cast by utterance of its wearer.

Components
In order to be cast, this spell required verbal and somatic components, as well as enough powdered obsidian to cover the caster's hands.

## Faith-magic zone

Faith-magic zone, or faith magic zone, was a divine abjuration spell that caused any non-divine magic to be less effective within a given area.

Description
The zone was not visible to any person or creature within the area of its casting. Only a small, silver radiance emanated from priests upon the spell's casting.

Effects
When cast, this spell created a sphere, 180 feet (58.9 meters) in diameter that slowed down any spells that were cast by wizards, sorcerers, warlocks, or other non-divine spellcasters. In addition, any magical items that drew upon arcane or other non-clerical spells functioned with less effectiveness.

Any people or creatures within the zone that were targeted by arcane spells or similar abilities gained significant protection against these types of magic.

The zone lasted for a few minutes and could be readily dispelled.

Components
In addition to verbal and somatic components, this spell required the holy symbol of the cleric casting it, as well as a vial of honey that had been collected from a honeycomb.

Usage
This spell was relatively well-known among the druids and other members of the Emerald Enclave.

A caster used this spell to heal another creature. After casting, the caster touched a targeted creature, who then was healed due to the flow of positive energy from the spell. The spell only functioned if the creature worshiped the same patron as the caster.

Components
This spell required only verbal and somatic components.

## Faroul

Faroul  was a human fortune hunter working as a jungle guide out of Port Nyanzaru, Chult in the late 15 century DR.

Description
Faroul was of Calishite descent. He stood at  tall and weighed . He carried a shortsword.

Personality
Coming from a rich family, Faroul was pompous, spoiled, overconfident in his own business acumen, and a dandy. He was accustomed to carousing and to freely spending money, and fancied himself an adventure-seeker. While he liked to tell stories about his prowess in combat, he was ultimately quite incompetent as both a guide and as a fighter, and would only draw his sword in emergencies.

Relationships
Faroul's relationship with his wealthy family was strained, and they had sent him away to Port Nyanzaru to earn his own fortune after becoming fed up with his antics.

His good friend, drinking companion, and partner as a jungle guide was Gondolo. The pair owned a flatulent triceratops named Zongo, whom they had purchased to participate in Port Nyanzaru's dinosaur races before discovering that it was hopelessly slow and only good for serving as a pack animal.

History
When Faroul was sent away to Port Nyanzaru by his parents, he was joined on the journey by Gondolo. Flush with Faroul's gold, they anticipated an easy life of partying, adventure, and making money. Within a few months, their lives of debauchery and gambling (especially at the dinosaur races) had left them not only broke, but in debt to several different bookies and more than one merchant prince.

Despite their haplessness, the pair then became jungle guides (thus working for the merchant prince Jobal), offering "two guides for the price of one" at a rate of 5 gold pieces per day with 30 days paid up front.

Out of desperation, Gondolo purchased a treasure map that showed the location of Needle's Bones, then sought to find a group of adventurers who would hire them as guides to "ancient sites and sacred places" where "treasure abounds". They asked for an equal share of any treasure from such an outing in exhange for a discount on their guide fees.

## Fairheight Range

The Fairheight Range, which were also called the Fairheight Mountains were a range of mountains along the northern border of the kingdom of Callidyrr on the island of Alaron in the Moonshaes.

Geography
The Fairheights were a rugged but relatively low mountain range that overlooked Whitefish Bay to the east and separated the southern lands of the Ffolk from the northern kingdom of Gnarhelm belonging to the Northmen. They were mined by both peoples and contained rich deposits of iron and precious metals including gold and silver as well as copper.

The tunnels beneath the mountains connected to Dwarvenhome, and from there, to the island of Gwynneth.

Climate
The mountain range served as a natural windbreak that kept the storms from the Trackless Sea confined to Gnarhelm, resulting in skies that were often overcast and rainy. Consequently, the weather in Callidyrr was usually more pleasant, perhaps further adding to the annoyance felt by the Northmen against their southern neighbors.

Notable Locations
 Blackstone: This busy mining town of the Ffolk was situated in the foothills of the Fairheights. As of the mid–14 century DR the town was producing some of the highest-quality iron in Faerûn. The miners, who were supervised by some dwarves, rewarded their own hard work with bouts of revelry paid for by the gold that was also gleaned from the generous mountains.

Inhabitants
The mountains fell within Callidyrr's county of Fairheight, and most of the Ffolk inhabitants were miners. On the northern side, the Northlander settlers were also miners, and both groups hired dwarves to oversee their operations.

The mountains were also home to independent dwarven clans, notably Clan Rookoath. They were joined by Clan Rustfire of Gwynneth during the 15 century DR.

The highlands of the Fairheights were infested with trolls that attacked those who were foolish enough to journey over the passes alone or in small numbers. Orcs and goblins from the mountains sometimes carried out raids against the mining towns along the foothills, but these settlements were not defenseless since they were protected by militias. As of the late 15 century DR, a shadow dragon had come to lead the orcs and trolls, as well as a number of shadow creatures, which it directed in a war to exterminate the dwarves and establish a new realm for it and its followers.

## Faerûn Under Siege

Dungeons & Dragons Dice Masters: Faerûn Under Siege is a game set in Forgotten Realms. It utilizes WizKids Games' proprietary Dice Building Game platform in which two players take the role of warlords, collect and assemble their "party" of adventurer dice and battle in head-to-head gameplay.

Gameplay
Each turn, players roll the dice to see what resources they have available, buy dice, send their party members into the field, and then strike at the enemy warlord. There are multiple cards available for each type of die so players get to choose which one to use. This lets players specialize dice to suit play style.

## Fate (rune)

Fate was a type of giant rune used for divination that could alter the luck of other creatures.

Effects
In order to activate this rune the wielder of the inscribed coin needed to desire to divine a specific individual's fate for the following date. This individual needed to be physically present for the reading and consent to it. Finally, the wielder needed to toss the coin into the air.

If the coin landed with the rune-inscribed side facing up, then the individual would experience greater luck the next day. If the coin landed with the rune-inscribed side facing down, then the individual would experience worse luck. If by chance the coin landed on its side, then something disastrous would befall the individual.

Components
Like most giant runes, it required no material components to cast. The process of shaping this rune generally took around one to eight minutes and it needed to be inscribed upon the side of a coin.

## Fachan

Fachan, also known as Gruumsh-kin, were a malevolent sentient species.

Description
The fachan was one of the most unusual-looking creatures on Toril. It had one leg and a single large foot with six toes. In the center of its face was a single large eye, and growing from the center of its chest was a single arm. Its face and skin resembled that of ogres and orcs. Their mouth was filled with sharp teeth, which were usually rotting and filthy.

Personality
Fachan were cruel creatures, often torturing their prey before eating them.

Abilities
Fachan had powerful muscles that allowed them to jump up to  vertically and  horizontally. Fachan had an excellent sense of hearing.

Combat
The primary battle tactic of a fachan was to ambush their target. They usually hid within the shallow water of their swamp, leaping out when their target was within range. Fachan could use most one-handed weapons but preferred axes, clubs, flails and swords. A fachan skull was unusually thick, and an impact could stun a target, even if the target was wearing armor. A bite from a fachan delivered a potent poison that paralyzed its victim. Once paralyzed, the fachan dragged the unfortunate victim underwater, holding them there until they drowned.

Society
Fachan lived communally as tribes. A typical fachan village was composed of up to thirty individuals living in six large structures called lodges, which resembled large beaver lodges. Each lodge was up to  in diameter and were camouflaged.

Ecology
Fachan were omnivores but their favorite meal was the meat from demihumans and humans.

Habitat
Fachan could be found living in marshes, swamps and wetlands south of the Orsraun Mountains. Fachan villages are known to be within the swamps of Durpar, Halruaa, and Turmish. The largest known fachan settlement was located within the Spider Swamp in Calimshan, although it was suspected even larger fachan settlements could be found within the swamps of Chult and Zakhara.

Uses
The feet of two fachan could be, if properly enchanted and treated, made into a pair of boots of striding and springing. The ear drum and canal of a fachan could be used as a material component in the brewing of a potion of clairaudience.

Appendix
Appearances

References

---

## False sensory input

False sensory input was a psionic power of the telepathy discipline that manipulated the senses of a subject.

Effects
The manifester altered one of the subject's senses—sight, hearing, smell, taste, or touch—replacing the specific information received and causing them to sense something different from the reality. For example, they could make a shut door appear open, a bird look like a book, a scream sound like wind howling, a slamming door sound like thunder or a cough, a bad smell seem fragrant, stale food taste fresh, oil taste like garlic; or a gentle touch feel like a painful blow. Furthermore, it could create disguises, such as making a person appear to be a specific different person or a different sex or race.

Only one sense could be so altered at a time, but this could be changed every several seconds. The apparent size, volume, or other "intensity" of something couldn't be changed by more than 50%: a castle could look like another castle or a hill of about the same height, but not like a peasant's shack; a human could not be mistaken for a bird; a scream could not be heard as a whisper; a slamming door not sound like rustling paper; oil could not taste like water, and so on. False sensory input couldn't invent a sensation where there was nothing, and it couldn't make a target oblivious to one where there was.

As false sensory input altered the subject's senses directly, it could even alter what they perceived from a true seeing spell and other psionic or magical means of acquiring information based on senses. However, the manifester needed to be aware of such a tactic ahead of time in order to deceive it.

A subject could resist with an act of will or other means of resisting psionic powers. Steadfast perception could give a subject a second chance to resist, but they needed to suspect a false sensory input to consider using it.

A false sensory input could distract opponent spellcasters and manifesters.

Only one subject at a time could be affected, but if an experienced manifester spent more psionic energy, they could affect additional subjects within  of one another. The false sensory input lasted as long as the manifester could maintain concentration on it, on the order of minutes. It operated at long range.

Prerequisite Powers
In early versions, manifesting the power required an initial contact and mindlink.

Displays
Manifesting false sensory input produced only a mental display.

Users
This power was knowable by psions who specialized as telepaths and spiritualists, as well as ardents and divine minds that followed the deception mantle.

This power was exhibited as an innate psionic ability amongst dhours.

In psionics terms, a false sensory input could be created by aboleths, ki-rin, and yuan-ti.

Notable Users
 Arvin, a human psion, who once tried to use false sensory input to trick a Serphidian.
 Gaulauntyr, an adult copper dragon.
 K'yorl Odran, a drow psionicist and the matron mother of House Oblodra.
 Vhondryl, a psionicist in Skullport and head of the Hall of Mental Splendor

Description
Faeril was a tall blonde-haired woman of athletic build. She preferred to wear the garb of green color.

Personality
Selflessly, Faeril was quick to raise her weapon and shield in aiding the good and defending her friends.

Abilities
Faeril was proficient in both combat and healing magic. Her combat reflexes and ability to cast spells in close combat often saved the lives of her and her friends.

Possessions
In 1369 DR, she went to battle armored with a magically enhanced +1 full plate and armed with a magical +3 warhammer.

She also carried her staff of flamestrike, a birch-carved magic quarterstaff that gave the user ability to cast flamestrike spell. When the Veiled Ones restored the ancient pumps of the House of Gems, they recovered an enchanted blade that once belonged to one of the members of the Sisterhood of Silver Fire. The Flame Blade was entrusted to Faeril, in respect of her religion.

History
In 1369 DR, Faeril, alongside a fellow cleric of Mystra Beriand joined the expedition to Myth Drannor, along with other adventurers who answered Elminster's call for aid against the Cult of the Dragon that planned to use the ruins' mythals and the Pools of Radiance to conquer the region.

Faeril's group was ambushed by orcs and a fire-breathing creature who slaughtered most of the adventurers. Thankfully, Faeril managed to drag the bling cellar away from danger. Together, the two survivors found shelter in Stillwater Ruins, behind the protection of an elven word of power. Faeril kept the blind cleric safe with her life.

Eventually, a group of adventurers from Phlan, the Veiled Ones, arrived at the ruins to join the forces opposing the cult. The heroes aided the survivors, and after they liberated the shrine of Mystra in Nightingale Court from dark nagas, Faeril joined the heroes in their quest to stop Kya Mordrayn and Pelendralaar.

## Faerie form

Faerie form was a spell known to specialty priests of Erevan Ilesere.

Effects
The spell functioned similarly to shapechange, but only allowed the caster to assume the form of different kinds of fey, such as a brownie, atomie, bramble, dobie, faerie fiddler, gorse, grig, killmoulis, nixie, pixie, quickling, sea sprite, or sprite. The caster adopted the abilities of that form except for innate magical abilities, magical resistances, and abilities based on intellect.

Additionally, the caster could change between faerie forms as long as the spell lasted, which could be up to a number of hours according to the caster's power. Each change in form only took a second.

Components
This spell required verbal, somatic, and material components, the last in the form of locks of hair from three different types of sprite.

## Fanged mask

A fanged mask was a magic half-mask adorned with sharp teeth that gave the wearer the ability to land devastating biting attacks in battle.

Description
To a person not versed in the Art, this half-mask appeared to be nothing more than one of the fanciful masks often worn by the nobility of Waterdeep at masquerade balls. This particular type of mask though had a row of sharp feline teeth at the bottom. The eyeholes of the mask had lenses, preventing the wearer from using other lensed objects at the same time.After the adventure's release, the item's description was adjusted with added lenses.

Powers
The wearer of a fanged mask was granted an ability to land devastating biting attacks in battle. The damage of the bite bore a +1 enchantment and could leave the bitee literally stunned for 6 seconds.

Creation
The creation of a fanged mask required knowledge of crafting wondrous items and a spell of spiritual weapon.

## Far reaching

The far reaching spells were alteration spells used by wizards.

Effects
Far reaching I allowed the caster to amplify the range of any beginner spell. Far reaching II doubled the range for beginner spells and increased the range for intermediate spells by a quarter. Far reaching III performed just like far reaching II but increased the range for intermediate spells by half.

Each casting of the far reaching spells would only affect a single spell cast within one minute. If more than a minute passed and the follow-up spell was not cast then the far reaching spell was wasted. These spells had no effect on "touch" spells.

Components
These spells required only verbal components to cast.

## Fang Rocks

The Fang Rocks, also rendered as Fangrocks, were a dangerous reef formation found in the Pirate Isles on the Sea of Fallen Stars.

Geography
The Fang Rocks were situated near the halfway point in the waters between Kelthann and Dragonisle, over the northeastern section of the Hmur Plateau.

Description
Most of the reef was only visible at low tide, making it particularly dangerous to incoming vessels unaware of the region's geography. They covered an area of sea roughly  in radius.

History
A fleet of twenty warships from Sembia was sent to destroy Immurk and his band of followers in the Pirate Isles in 1180 DR. Unaware of the hidden Fang Rocks, all but three of the warships were destroyed by the reef.

## Falias Emberthumb

Falias Emberthumb was a halfling merchant who sold mundane goods, cloth, and dresses from Waterdeep in the city of Daggerford circa 1372 DR.

Description
Falias Emberthumb was a portly halfling with a bold head, crow-like nose, dandy grommet mustache, and a small beard.

Personality
He was a nervous fellow who often sweated profusely when stressed, wrangling his hands. He was very polite and proper, and bowed according to customs even when interacting with his own employees.

Possessions
Falias Emberthumb's regular caravan consisted of three wagons drawn by six oxen.

History
In 1372 DR, Falias Emberthumb hired mercenaries to accompany his caravan from Waterdeep to Daggerford, a shipment of ladies' gowns. One of the hirelings was an experienced fighter, Talarenne, and the other would later become the hero of Daggerford, and another. They traveled along the Trade Way until the caravan was stopped by a recently fallen tree. This was a scene of an ambush by a group of mysterious bandits (in reality, Zhentarim agents). As the attackers were defeated, Falias assumed the attacks be simple foolish robbers who foolishly attacked a shipment of clothes.

Reaching the safety of Daggerford's walls, Falias made sure his surviving injured guard received the best care. The halfling merchant felt honor-bound to offer the hero of Daggerford one of his best dresses as a reward for the job well done, and opened up a stall in the city's marketplace, not far from the Daggerford Castle.

## Fallen Giant Tomb

The Fallen Giant Tomb was a  tall statue of a mountain giant carved out of a stone ridge in the Western Heartlands.

Geography
This landmark was situated roughly between the Drawn Swords and Skull Gorge.

History
At some point centuries prior to 1366 DR, a great mountain giant chieftain died whose name has been lost to the annals of time. Grieving, his followers carved a semblance of his sleeping form out of a stone ridge.

According to detailed stories later told by dwarves, elves, and giants — those mountain giants buried their chief's body beneath the stone ridge. They laid him to rest in a suit of everbright steel plate mail, atop a bed of stacked golden ingots, with his magical warhammer by his side. There was estimated to be at least two thousand or more of those golden ingots and they were said to be so large that each was worth a hundred gold pieces.

Over time the entrance to his tomb became totally obscured, yet still many attempted expeditions to uncover it.

Centuries after its construction, people found that if any blood was spilled upon either the statue or the slopes of its ridge — even if it was from uncooked meat or a pre-existing wound — then four or more skeletons of mountain giants would arise. They would be armed with spiked clubs and seek to slay any living creature that did not flee from the ridge immediately. They would also arise whenever anyone dug on the ridge. There was seemingly no limit to the amount of skeletons that could erupt from the ground — around 1366 DR, one elderly ranger claimed to Volothamp Geddarm that over the years he had slain over thirty skeletons from that ridge.

By 1366 DR, a group of three war parties had allegedly managed to find his tomb and stole the chief's magical warhammer, but were unable to steal any of his gold due to being driven off by the tomb's undead guardians.

Rumors & Legends
Some legends claimed that on certain nights this statue would rise up and slay anyone nearby, though adventurers who had camped by the tomb discredit this tale.

## Faldis

Faldis was an accomplished and venerated elven warrior and member of Akh'Velahr who died before the advent of the Weeping War and was buried with honors in the depths of the Dwarven Dungeons of Myth Drannor.

Possessions
Faldis's Defender was his enchanted stout platinum shield that gave its owner magical courage.

History
In life, Faldis was considered to be heroic and courageous. In reality he was neither. He commissioned a platinum shield to be enchanted to compensate for his flaws. The shield came to be known as Faldis's Defender and brought him the acclaim and status. At the end of his life, the warrior hidden his shield, the most precious and the most shameful possession in the secret room inside what later to become his tomb.

In 1369 DR, a group of adventurers from Phlan discovered the fallen warrior's diary and his deathbed confession about the nature of his achievements and acclaims asking however finds his shield to use if for truly heroic and courageous deeds.

Faldis's Defender was enchanted to give its wearer courage even if they possessed none prior to donning the shield. Additionally, the magic shield increased the user's armor, defense, and initiative.

History
Faldis's Defender accompanied its owner, Faldis throughout his career, leading the elf to the hero status and to being buried with honors inside the Deep Halls of Dwarven Dungeons. Before his demise, Faldis wrote a confession in his diary and hid the shield inside the secret room inside his crypt. This was where the shield remained until 1369 DR.

## Faroz

Faroz was a caravan master who lived in the nation of Turmish during the late 15  century DR.

Description
He was an experienced leader, and maintained good discipline among his caravaneers.

Relationships
Faroz had a strong ally in his second-in-command Majeed, and was well-protected a number of wizards skilled in battle magic.

History
In the, Faroz and his caravan came across four travelers, chosen of four different deities, and offered them a place within his caravan as they traveled across the Vilhon Reach. They were on a mission from the gods to deliver the Eye of Gruumsh to the Underchasm.

The column was harried by orc assaults, and made a brief stop in Xorhun, to hire more guards. After passing the River Arrabar on its way to Ormpetarr, the caravan made camp in the shell of a ruined fortress, sitting atop a hill that overlooked the Chondalwood. Faroz and his people were besieged again, by Yder Tanthul and his Night Guard, until their passengers departed into the Chondalwood.

Fahren had a good relationship with both types of his students. However, the thieves he taught used his spells to rob others.

History
Fahren was born in . He was credited with the invention of two spells: darkness, 15' radius and glitterdust. Fahren died very young at the age of 40 in, making him one of the shortest-lived arcanists of the era, along with Efteran.

## Fanraet

Overseer Fanraet was one of the leaders of the Cult of the Dragon in the ruins of Myth Drannor in 1369 DR.

History
In 1369 DR, Fanraet oversaw the excavations and research of the ruins of the Room of Words in Onaglym of Myth Drannor. Lieutenant Iolrak was directly reporting to the Overseer of the mission's progress. Even though there was not much progress in the recovery of the Word of Oblivion, his cultists were able to collect many other glyphs and words of power that opened previously sealed areas within the Dwarven Dungeons, Elven Catacombs, and other parts of Myth Drannor.

However, for his failures, Overseer Fanraet was beheaded by Grand Overseer Vidorus as per Kya Mordrayn's orders. The unlucky overseer's head was sent as a dire warning to Overseer Sinod.

## Fairy ice

Fairy ice was a weather phenomenon that was unique to the Great Glacier and the Endless Ice Sea.

Description
Fairy ice was a form of precipitation, made of minuscule granular ice spheres with the consistency of slush. On the ground it resembled regular snow, the only difference was that fairy ice shimmered like a rainbow in sunlight.

Fairy ice settled in depressions in the terrain, and it was common for entire crevasses and valleys to be filled with it. Due to its granular density, fairy ice was similar to quicksand. An individual who stepped onto fairy ice began slowly sinking, but could be pulled out or pull themselves out, otherwise they sank down until completely submerged and began suffocating.

Locations
 The innugaakalikurit village of Gomwemk had a trench surrounding the village that was filled with fairy ice, utilizing it as a defensive measure.
 The Ice Spires sported fairy ice atop them.
 The tallest peaks of the Keryjek Ridge glimmered due to fairy ice deposits.
 The Shakkak Pit in Nakvaligach was filled completely with fairy ice.

Appendix

 Quicksand, a similar natural hazard composed of sand.

The goodly archwizard established his enclave over Dagger's Point in  and decided to clear the city of the evil that pervaded it. Trothas Helgaras ruled Dagger's Point, which had high levels of assassinations and robberies. Trothas found out that Falious came to cleanse his city, and commanded his men (under disguise of his previous students) to infiltrate Tanathras. ## Falcon's hunting lodge

Falcon's hunting lodge was the only safe retreat for travelers in the southern reaches of Neverwinter Wood during the late 15 century DR. It belonged to Gustaf Stellern, who was called Falcon the Hunter. He catered to wealthy people from Neverwinter who were looking for a break from city life. Falcon employed the cook Corwin and the stablehand Pell.

Description
The main lodge was a two-story building with the heads of dead orcs hanging as trophies on the walls of the dining hall. A fortified tower was attached to the main house. Additionally, the compound included a guest house, a connected two-story stable house and smithy, a gatehouse and an outhouse. There were also pens on the hunting lodge grounds for two dozen chickens and a rooster, six pigs, and four goats. In his pantry, Falcon stored enough supplies for an entire season. The whole compound was protected against the forest wildlife by a  log palisade. A narrow stone bridge allowed crossing the Neverwinter River at the hunting lodge.

Geography

The hunting lodge was located in the south of the Neverwinter Wood, on the east bank of a tributary of the Neverwinter River.

Services
Food and stays in the simple guest house were free but a stay in the deluxe guest room cost 10 gp per night. Falcon also offered his survival skills to anybody in need of a guide.

History
Following the appearance of the white dragon Cryovain in the Sword Mountains, Falcon noticed an alliance of orcs from the south and the half-orc anchorites of Talos had gathered in the Woodland Manse not far from his hunting lodge. He reached out to Phandalin to hire some adventurers to make a preemptive strike against them. In retaliation, a band of twenty orcs attacked Falcon's hunting lodge. The anchorites of Talos also conjured Gorthok the Thunder Boar to aid their attack.

## Faith Killer

The Faith Killer was an enchanted axe created by the power of sheer will and denial of divine powers.

Description
The Faith Killer appeared to be an ordinary one-handed axe.

Powers
The axe possessed a +2 enchantment. It had a small chance of dispelling magic upon hit and granted the user a small amount of resistance to magic.

If a lawful-aligned creature not native to the Prime Material plane picked up the Faith Killer, it almost certainly suffered lightning damage dealt to them by the enchanted weapon.

History
Sages postulated that the enchantments of the Faith Killer were born out of its original owner's, Erion the Skeptic, disbelief in gods and divine magic. Erion was one of very few on Toril who completely denied any and all deities and their devotees. He gained notoriety trying to exposed clerics as charlatans and that divine magic was nothing more than arcane or some other type of magic unrelated to gods. Sages believed that Erion's conviction was so strong, it created sympathetic magical powers that were imbued onto the Faith Killer. Erion the Skeptic's file was cut short by clergy of Selûne who were forced to kill him in self-defense. The Faith Killer was taken by the priests, and as for Erion himself, it was believed his soul became the part of Myrkul's Bone Castle.

By 1281 DR, the Faith Killer found its way into the hands of Conlan, the proprietor of Kuldahar's blacksmith shop.

## Fastred

Fastred was an infamous bandit chieftain who was based in the Vast Swamp between Cormyr and Sembia hundreds of years before the 14 century DR.

History
Many histories of Cormyr made mention of Fastred, both in history and local legend, such as Carcroft the Long's Anthropologic and Folkloric Historie of the Settled Lands.

In his day, Fastred commanded a gang of brigands and killers who regularly preyed on the caravans that crossed the Way of the Manticore. They slew all those who would not surrender and looted the caravans of their riches like gold, silver, and gems before escaping back into the Vast Swamp. Fastred used his exceptional knowledge of the swampland and its treacherous terrain to evade pursuers, even when small armies sought him, leaving hunters caught in bogs and drowned. He seemed to have little trouble with the other denizens of the Vast Swamp, such as the goblins, lizardfolk, trolls, and grells.

Thus he lived like a king, in security and riches, until at last he faced his death, from natural causes, some time in the mid-to-late 9 century DR. Fastred bequeathed half his wealth to his followers to be divided up among them, while he kept the remainder for himself in death. Then Fastred was sealed inside his tomb in the Vast Swamp. His followers seemingly went their separate ways, and shared something of Fastred's story.

In the years after, a legend arose that Fastred's ghost lingered, guarding his treasure still against any who would seek or stumble across it. It became one of the most dreaded of the Vast Swamp's many threats.

Sightings of a mysterious glowing axe-wielding figure said to be Fastred's ghost became common in the farmland around the town of Ghars, northwest of the Vast Swamp, in the, starting in late Mirtul. It had townsfolk and farmers fearful and caused business at the nearby Swamp Rat tavern to decline. In fact, the ghost was nothing more than blacksmith's assistant Dovo in old armor, carrying a large axe, and smeared all over with glowing glimmergrass paint. His beheaded body was found on Eleint 17, and the real Fastred's ghost was initially considered as a possible suspect, with the motive being vengeance against the imitator, but he was eventually ruled out after various mundane clues were found. Meanwhile, the adventuress Kendra had come to Ghars while hunting Fastred's treasure. Grodoveth, a king's envoy, had researched Fastred in the Ghars library and given Dovo the idea as a cover for their spying operation. He was even able to locate Fastred's tomb itself. The next night, Grodoveth went into the tomb and was beheaded by Fastred's axe, which had been set as a trap in the tomb. Local retired War Wizard Benelaius also located the tomb, went there, hid while Grodoveth went in, and planted evidence framing the treacherous Mayor Tobald and concealed the trap again, leaving the axe on the floor. He also took Fastred's treasure.

Then, after midnight on Eleint 20, the true ghost of Fastred finally appeared in person at the edge of the Vast Swamp and advanced on Tobald as he fled justice at Benelaius's cottage. Fastred only watched as Tobald backed into a mire and was sucked down to drown, before fading away. Later, Benelaius and Jasper returned to Fastred's tomb to discuss what really transpired there. However, Fastred's bones—still in the left-open tomb and likely possessed by a wandering evil spirit—reanimated as a skeleton and pursued them out of the tomb and through the swamp, until it was struck by the darts of a thornslinger plant and destroyed. After that, Benelaius presumed that Fastred haunted the swamp no more.

Benelaius later arranged for Fastred's treasure to be paid to Dovo's widow and children, via a fiction of an inheritance from a distant relative.

Fastred's Tomb

Although hidden, Fastred's tomb wasn't especially hard to find, with sufficient research into local folklore, history, and geography. It lay within a rocky island within the Vast Swamp, accessed via an unlocked trapdoor covered in moss. Slime-slicked stone stairs led down into a subterranean chamber, where Fastred's own axe was mounted in a trap to be hurled at the first person to step foot inside and trip the cord across the floor.

From here, a  tunnel carved by water led to a much-larger cavern. Here, atop a dais, Fastred's armored body sat in a throne, decayed to bare bones, rotting wood, and rusted iron, with cushions lost to mold by the mid-1300s DR. His treasure chest sat on the ground at his feet. Runes carved into the wall above the throne read Fastred's epitaph.

Description
In life and in death, Fastred was a giant of a man, being tall, broad of body and thick of limb. He wore a gleaming mail shirt and carried a huge axe. As a ghost, he had long hair and a deathly look of sunken cheeks, grim mouth, and disturbing eyes, and he emanated a cold blue glow.

Possessions
He was notorious for his enormous battleaxe, said to be able to cut people in two.

Fadlimi posed as a young and beautiful human woman of slender build and dark hair.

Using this guise in Suzail, Fadlimi plied a retired scout of the Purple Dragons, Ingo Siegur, for information, by getting the old man to become infatuated with her and persistently asking for tales of his military career. Farel's gold bullets were created out of gold and possessed two enchantments: upon collision they released acid, dealing additional damage, and had a coin-toss chance of affecting the target with the slow spell for twelve seconds.

Farel's gold bullets were invented by Farel Harod of Amn during one of his lengthy expeditions into a deadly dungeon. He ran out of bullets and decided to use his gold coins instead, somehow he realized that he preferred them to regular bullets. After he escaped the perils of the dungeon he commissioned improved version of these expensive bullets. Since then, Farel's gold bullets have been appearing all over place, including the city of Neverwinter, Sword Coast, and the Moonsea.

Fayr's halberd made a name for itself being wielded by Bedwyn Fayr. His adventuring company employed clever tactics of sending dwarf and halfling members into close combat while taller adventurers used halberds and bows from a distance. The group was active through the early parts of the 13 century DR until the group was almost killed in 1252 DR. The company's two survivors, Fayr and Pep Greenfields retired their adventuring mantles and opened an antique shop in Waterdeep.

Thirty years later, by 1281 DR, Fayr's halberd found its way into the stock of Conlan's Smithy in Kuldahar in Icewind Dale.

## Fai Chen

Fai Chen  was a planewalking merchant who ran the Fantastical Faire, a traveling shop. As part of a gambit to get out of servitude under the rakshasa Mahadi, he drew on obscure knowledge of ki and made duplicates of himself.

Description
Fai Chen was a mustached Shou man standing  tall with weathered tan skin, short-cropped black hair, and dark green eyes. He typically wore somewhat worn out monk's robes covered in dust.

Personality
As befitting of a merchant, Fai Chen acted friendly, had good manners, and was preternaturally aware of what was happening in and around his shop. He didn't allow his merchandise to even be touched until a full transaction had taken place, and would always hand purchased wares to the buyer himself.

Fai liked gaming sets, particularly card games, and was always happy to trade his wares for them.

Although not always apparent, the most unusual aspect of Fai was that there was more than one of him. When multiple Fais were around each other, they tended to give one another knowing looks and would finish each other's sentences.

Abilities
Well-versed in the study of ki, Fai Chen devised of a way to use this power in order to create duplicates of himself. Rather than the better-known clones created by the clone spell, these doubles remained extensions of Fai himself, and although their shared knowledge wasn't flawless, none of them were different people.

Possessions
The wooden cart Fai Chen traveled with functioned as his home as well as his shop. When selling wares, he could open the latches at the top of the cart in order for the split ceiling to fall to the sides in order to display a selection of magic items hanging from pegs and hooks. It was rumored that the cart was some manner of extradimensional space, but this was unverified, and Fai Chen did not want to speak about it.

Fai also owned a monocle that could discern the true value of any observed item, as well as a dagger made out of a large tooth of unknown origin.

Relationships
Gary, the mule pulling the Fantastical Faire cart, and Drandeldew, the blue faerie dragon familiar, were Fai's constant companions.

Fai and Mahadi had a contentious relationship, as Fai was the only mortal to ever escape from one of Mahadi's contracts. Because of this, Mahadi simultaneously admired Fai's resourcefulness and also hated him intensely.

Su Zhen Kang and Fai were once good friends as monks in the same order, but when the two became indentured under Mahadi, Su Zhen was unable to escape and ended up signing his soul away to the rakshasa. Twisted by his service as Mahadi's master torturer, Su Zhen became emotionless, and had no compunctions about harming his former friend in order to learn of his self-multiplication technique and free himself from bondage.

Fai had a shrewd cousin called Mei Fun who sometimes accompanied him on business trips.

The planetar Rinzel was familiar enough with Fai to recognize him by looks alone, and seemingly didn't have a high opinion of him.

History
Fai Chen was originally a temple monk. While mastering the ki manipulation techniques of his order, he and his fellow monk Su Zhen Kang grew weary of their monastic life and yearned for adventure. As the duo read about grand adventures, they attracted the attention of Mahadi, who offered them a deal to learn to travel the planes with his Wandering Emporium and eventually, after paying off their incurred debt, to go free with this knowledge.

As it turned out, the only plane that the Wandering Emporium wandered was the Nine Hells. What's more, the monks were trapped in servitude, as their debt to Mahadi kept growing with every meal he gave them, every monster in their path he disposed of, and every break he granted. On top of that, accounting for interest, there was seemingly no way to get out from under this contract. Eventually, Su Zhen broke from despair, and ended up selling his soul to Mahadi in exchange for power and the possibility of happiness, devoting himself fully to Asmodeus. Fai, however, held on to his soul, and looked for another way out.

Fai Chen found a loophole in the contract, and exploited it by drawing upon his obscure knowledge of ki in order to create duplicates of himself. As long as at least one of these duplicates continued to toil under Mahadi, the rest were free to go out and find external revenue to pay off the debt, which Fai and his doubles did by building up their merchant empire. Ultimately, their debt was paid off, and the Fais returned to the Prime Material plane to continue their trade in Faerûn.

Rumors & Legends
False stories of Fai Chen's early life circulated. Many believed that the reason he left his monastic order was due to expulsion for unknown offenses. Another rumor held that he joined a group of adventurers that traveled across Faerûn looking for riches and danger as a way of putting his days of asceticism in the past. One anecdote in particular made the claim that a horrible encounter with a mummy was what made Fai give up adventuring and become a traveling merchant instead.

## Fane of Lolth (Northdark)

The Fane of Lolth was a underground temple dedicated to the Queen of Spiders located beneath Rothé Valley on the Sword Coast North.

Location
It could be found in the depths beneath Toril known as the Edge of the Underdark, the passageways and caves that were situated above the Northdark.

Structure
The simple temple comprised a stone-floored hall with a vaulted ceiling, branching off from drow-dug tunnels and subterranean outposts.

Interior
On either side of the temple's walls were a series of stone statues that each depicted the likeness of a drow priestess carrying a staff in one hand, as her other was set forward as if to receive an item. Six lit braziers were set between the statues, five of which held blue flames while the sixth contained natural, orange-red fire.

At the head of the temple was a simple shrine that comprised three more statues of drow priestesses, each facing what appeared to be a sacrificial altar. The shrine was set within an ominous-looking piece of drow metalwork that took the form of man-sized pincers adorned with engravings and gems of violet hue.

Activities
The temple was a place of worship where drow could properly pay tribute to the Spider-Queen Lolth. It also housed at least one history book that outlined the ancient history of the Araushnee-worshiping "dhaerow", previously known as the Ilythiiri, including their exile to the Underdark.

History
The temple was raided in the, by the Hero of Sleeping Dragon Bridge as they carried out their execution of Prince Sarkon'Iss.

## Faerie fiddler

Faerie fiddlers were an odd and obscure species of fey.

Description
Faerie fiddlers resembled extraordinarily thin, withered old men only  tall, always dressed in somber and archaic clothes. They always had a minute, wonderfully crafted fiddle.

Personality
Faerie fiddlers were perpetually cheerful creatures who were lively and spry despite their aged appearance. Their only motivation was to protect other members of their community and to make the world a little more pleasant.

Abilities
Faerie fiddlers had a number of natural magical abilities, which they channeled through their music. The least of their magical tunes was one that they often wove into other tunes, a tune that prevented those who danced to it from experiencing hunger, thirst, or fatigue while dancing. A more forceful tune was similar to Otto's irresistible dance, and the most powerful tune they could play was similar to time stop: for every hour spent dancing, a year would pass in the outside world. This could only be played during the full moon and was reserved for use against those people who offended the faeries greatly without overtly attacking them.

Faerie fiddlers themselves were immune to all charm and enchantment magic. They knew all the languages of faeriekind as well.

Combat
Faerie fiddlers never started fights, but protected themselves and their companions with their magic if one broke out.

Society
Faerie fiddlers were curious fey, considered by some to be icons of fey society. They always lived in a community with other fey, but there was never more than one fiddler in any community.

Trivia
Priests of Erevan Ilesere were capable of assuming the form of a faerie fiddler through use of the spell faerie form.

## Favor of Yathaghera

Favor of Yathaghera was a divine alteration spell that gave wings to horses and used by specialty priests devoted to Lurue called Silvermaids.

Effects
This spell caused an equine target, including a centaur, to sprout the wings of a pegasus for several hours, allowing them to fly. The spell also made the spellcaster proficient with handling the flying mount for the duration of the spell but the target could only be ridden bareback. Any saddle placed upon the target would simply fall off. The steed behaved as if it were born with the wings.

If used on an equine target that already had wings, such as a pegasus, then the target's flying speed was doubled and their aerial maneuverability was enhanced.

Components
In addition to verbal and somatic components, this spell required as material components a feather from a pegasus and the priestess's holy symbol to cast.

## Favor of Valkur

Favor of Valkur was a divine enchantment spell used by specialty priests devoted to Valkur to protect sailors at sea.

Effects
This spell ensured that a sailor who fell overboard would float safely to shore via favorable winds and currents regardless of the weather conditions. Furthermore, the sailor would reach shore without drowning or dying of thirst or hunger. A recipient could only receive the favor of Valkur once during their lifetime. The spell would end either when the favor was fulfilled or one year after its casting.

Components
In addition to verbal components and somatic components, this spell required as material components a piece of driftwood from a ship that sank at sea to cast.

This plate mail belonged to Captain Fallorain, the leader of Calimshan's 12 cavalry brigade. The commander wore this enchanted armor to the Battle of the Spider Swamp, where he and his comrades met their doom battling some evil whose name was lost to history.

## Favor of Tymora

Favor of Tymora, known also as Tymora's smile, was a divine abjuration spell used by priests devoted to Tymora, enabling them to confer a protection on a single target.

Effects
Once cast, the favor of Tymora could not be dispelled and would last until the target's death or the spell's energy had been expended. This spell would cancel the bane of Beshaba spell. The protection offered from this spell made it easier for the target to resist the effects of spells, magic items, poisons, etc. That resistance became slightly weaker with each use, slowly draining the spell's magic until none remained. ## Fanatics of the Overflowing Pit

The Fanatics of the Overflowing Pit were an elite order of drow Ghaunadauran crusaders.

Activities
This organization waged warfare on the clergies of rival faiths.

Base of Operations
In the 14 century DR this organization was based in the city of Llurth Dreier, where they made up part of the city's armed forces.

History
This crusader organization traced its origins back to the Ilythiir, when that empire was at its height. Following the fall of Ilythiir and the drow race's exile into the Underdark by the Seldarine, an event known as the "Descent of the Drow", the organization went silent.

The organization was long rumored to still survive in the city of Llurth Dreier, but it did not truly resurface until the year, in the conflict known as the Webfire Wars. Following the end of that conflict, the organization would continue to operate in the southern Underdark for millennia.

## Faces of Madness

The Faces of Madness were seven artifacts of ancient Imaskar. Each of them was a mask themed after a school of magic. They were intended to be used to overthrow Imaskar and achieve unlimited power, but the Mulan Rebellion prevented their creators from taking full advantage of their powers.

Powers
Each of the Faces of Madness carried great power over a school of magic, and bore a horrifying curse that only the creator of each mask knew how to bypass.

History
The Cabal of Madness was an alliance of Imaskari archmages in pursuit of power without limits. They gained the knowledge of how to build artifacts from horrors beyond their world, and over decades, constructed several masks of such power. The masks were finished before, but not soon enough to allow the Cabal to carry out its plans: the Mulan rebelled, bolstered by the powers of their pantheons arriving that year. The masks became lost in the resulting tumult. One of them resurfaced in, carried by Kujawa to Thakos.

By the late 14 century, they were sought by a number of groups, among them the Harpers, the Red Wizards, mages from Halruaa, the Zhentarim, and the Tel'Teukiira. Though their number was not known with certainty, knowledge of seven of them was still available at that time. They were considered to be the Imaskarcana by some sources, but later and more complete ones put them in a category known as the False Imaskarcana.

Artifacts
Dhonas's Shroud
Ynaerv's Mask
Mask of Aberration
Mask of Shum
Shade's Veil
The Facade
The Master's Face

## Fatemakers

The Fatemakers were a radical band of priests who believed all luck deities were one and the same, such as Tymora, Beshaba, Erevan Ilesere, and so on. They also followed the philosophy that anyone could influence fate and luck in the same way as these deities. Moreover, they believed that if given enough power mortals could become their own gods. When approached for guidance, it was not unusual for Fatemakers to issue commands rather than advice in an attempt to determine the day-to-day affairs of everyone they could.

Many Tymorans despised the Fatemakers, rightly suspecting that many of them were corrupt and secret followers of Beshaba.
